# THE ÆTHERIUM CODEX — System Reference Document
## Prima Materia — Framework, Principles & Material Science

*Alpha SRD — Second Draft*

---

## FOUNDATIONAL PRINCIPLES

### The Inseparability Principle

An item's alchemical specification IS its mechanical specification. The dual periodic table entry for a material IS its stat block. A designer does not decide that a steel longsword deals a certain damage tier — the real periodic table specifies that steel's tensile strength places it there. An alchemist does not decide that obsidian has a Borderlands heritage — the material's volcanic origin determines its planar passport.

The system reads the world. It does not impose upon it.

### The Universal System Principle

The same framework — spell sentence grammar, isotope profiles, interaction rules, hazard classifications — operates identically at every scale. A chemical reaction in a crucible, a mutation expressing in a blade, a practitioner composing a rote, a planar bleed zone forming across a region — all are governed by the same mechanics. The scale changes. The system does not.

### The Standards Commitment

Material properties derive from real-world measurements. Hardness from the Mohs scale. Tensile strength from measured MPa. Hazard classifications from NFPA 704 and GHS standards. Published real-world data can be plugged directly into the system because the system was built to conform to those standards, not approximate them.

---

## THE FIVE PLANES

Five planar engines underlie all magical and material reality. Each is a complex system governing a domain of existence. None reduce to a single concept.

### The Borderlands — The Engine of Matter

The Borderlands governs physical substance and its transformations — elemental interaction, phase transition, material properties, environmental forces, and the behavior of matter under pressure, temperature, and chemical reaction. It encompasses fire, water, earth, air, and every state that emerges from their combinations. The Borderlands IS the physical environment.

Phase Wheel: Earth (Solid/Static) / Water (Liquid/Cycling) / Air (Gas/Projecting) / Fire (Plasma/Absorbing)
Diagonal Pairs: Earth ↔ Air / Water ↔ Fire

### The Verdant Font — The Engine of Biological Process

The Verdant Font governs biological systems in their full complexity: growth, adaptation, consumption, propagation, mutation, symbiosis, predation, parasitism, disease, immune response, and ecological interaction. It is NOT "life" in any simplified sense — it includes the fungal network consuming a dead tree, the virus hijacking a host cell, the predator consuming prey, and the cancer growing without regulation.

Phase Wheel: Flora (Solid/Static) / Fauna (Liquid/Cycling) / Myco (Gas/Projecting) / Viral (Plasma/Absorbing)
Diagonal Pairs: Flora ↔ Myco / Fauna ↔ Viral

### The Synod of Endings — The Engine of Completion

The Synod governs endings, preservation, permanence, information storage, identity persistence, and the process by which all systems reach their most stable final state. It is NOT "death" — it is the administrative engine that processes what death produces. The Synod absorbed the functions of both the Silent Athenaeum (forbidden knowledge and preserved secrets) and the Concordance (interplanar diplomacy and transit), making it the Plane of Record, Judgment, and Interplanar Transit. Its domain includes the physical remains after biological process ceases, the archival record of what existed, the spectral persistence of identity beyond physical function, and the entropic void that claims what cannot be preserved. The Synod operates as an infinite bureaucracy — the Triad of Administration (Vestus managing data, Nomos managing process, Moros managing physicality) runs the plane as a processing engine for the cosmos. The Synod is patient. It asserts that everything will eventually reach its most stable state. It is always correct.

Phase Wheel: Osseous (Solid/Static) / Archival (Liquid/Cycling) / Spectral (Gas/Projecting) / Void (Plasma/Absorbing)
Diagonal Pairs: Osseous ↔ Spectral / Archival ↔ Void

### The Hidden Forge — The Engine of Technology

The Hidden Forge governs the processes by which incompatible things are made to work together — mediation, catalysis, engineering, and deliberate problem-solving through applied technique. The Forge has no native products and no inherent expression. It is not a reagent — it is the engineer holding the crucible in the right position. The Forge has no internal antithetical states because making incompatible things compatible is its fundamental nature. All sixteen of its internal isotope combinations are natural. The Forge is the universal mediator: the only plane that can interface with all others without being captured by any of them.

Phase Wheel: Pneuma (Gas/Projecting) / Volt (Plasma/Absorbing) / Chem (Liquid/Cycling) / Crystal (Solid/Static)
Diagonal Pairs: None — all combinations are natural within the Forge

### The Palimpsest — The Engine of Superposition

The Palimpsest governs probability, narrative determination, and the space where choices exist before they are made — fate, luck, historical echo, temporal perception, and the process by which contested outcomes become resolved. The Palimpsest produces no physical effects on its own. Every physically impactful Palimpsest expression requires a secondary plane to give the probability a body. What the Palimpsest determines is WHICH of the possible outcomes actually occurs. Mortals attribute the Palimpsest as the source of learned magic, but it is more accurately the filter through which the deeper, unnamed source of raw fate is accessed — a filter mortals require because direct contact with unfiltered probability would be overwhelming.

Phase Wheel: Echo/Resolved (Solid/Static) / Veil/Handed (Liquid/Cycling) / Probability/Contested (Gas/Projecting) / Glitch/Suspended (Plasma/Absorbing)
Diagonal Pairs: Echo ↔ Probability (Resolved ↔ Contested) / Veil ↔ Glitch (Handed ↔ Suspended)

---

## PLANAR INTERACTIONS

### Formation Types

When planar expressions combine, the formation type determines stability and crafting requirements.

**◆ Natural Combinations** form without external catalyst under appropriate conditions. Adjacent expressions on the phase wheel cooperate or coexist. All Hidden Forge internal combinations are natural.

**⚙ Catalyzed Diagonal Combinations** are competing states within the same plane. Fire and Water, Earth and Air — these annihilate toward one or the other without sustained energy input. In nature, the environment provides that input (steam exists at geysers). In crafting, Forge mediation provides it. The ⚙ designation tells the alchemist: this compound needs ongoing stabilization.

**◆⚙ Emulsion Combinations** force expressions from antithetical planes together through a Hidden Forge catalyst. The two antithetical pairs are Verdant Font ↔ Synod (the Biotic Paradox) and Borderlands ↔ Palimpsest (the Reality Paradox). Emulsions require three-domain knowledge: both antithetical planes plus the Forge. The catalyst type (Crystal, Chemical, Pneuma, or Volt) determines how the antithetical tension is maintained.

### Behavioral Outcomes

When expressions interact, the result falls into one of four categories:

**Cooperative** — the expressions reinforce each other. The combination amplifies what both already do. Produces stronger output with minimal instability.

**Competing** — the expressions conflict. Each pushes toward a different outcome. The combination requires negotiation — one may dominate, or both may persist in tension.

**Cancellation** — the expressions annihilate. Without mediation, one destroys the other or both are consumed. Antithetical expressions without Forge mediation produce cancellation.

**Emergent** — the combination produces something neither expression would produce alone. The most dramatic and least predictable outcome. Emergent results are why alchemy is simultaneously the most valuable and most dangerous discipline.

---

## THE ISOTOPE SYSTEM

### What Isotopes Are

Each plane contains sixteen isotope states — four pure expressions and twelve compounds formed from pairs of the four phase elements. These isotopes are the specific planar expressions that materials embody, that practitioners learn to work with, and that items manifest as mutations or installed effects.

Each isotope profile specifies which Effects it can naturally produce, which it categorically excludes, how its expression scales with Formation Stage, and what its default behavior is. A material's isotope identity determines what it CAN do — not through arbitrary assignment but because that combination of phase elements produces those specific physical or metaphysical behaviors.

### Learning Isotopes

A practitioner learns individual isotopes as discrete talents, each ranked 1-10. Pure elements (the four cardinal expressions of each plane) are prerequisite foundations — a practitioner must understand constituent pures before working with compound isotopes. Catalyzed diagonal isotopes require additional Forge knowledge. Emulsion isotopes require knowledge from both antithetical planes plus the Forge.

### Interaction Profiles

Each isotope inherits a behavioral profile from its dominant phase:

| Phase | Profile | Behavior |
|---|---|---|
| Solid | Static | Persists without change. Holds position. Resists transformation. |
| Liquid | Cycling | Transforms what passes through it. Processes, converts, redistributes. |
| Gas | Projecting | Pushes outward. Disperses, spreads, broadcasts. |
| Plasma | Absorbing | Draws inward. Consumes, converts to fuel, self-sustains through consumption. |

Compound isotopes carry a dominant profile from their primary phase and a behavioral character from their recessive phase.

---

## FORMATION STAGE

Formation Stage (1-10) rates the intensity at which a material's planar expression has crystallized. It simultaneously represents quality, purity, and the depth of planar adaptation the material has undergone.

### The Three Bands

Formation Stage bands align with NFPA 704 hazard severity and proficiency thresholds:

| Band | Formation Stage | NFPA Equivalent | Expression Level | Access |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Minor | 1-4 | 0-1 | Cosmetic to mild. Detectable but not dangerous. Safe with basic precautions. | Novice |
| Moderate | 5-7 | 2-3 | Combat-relevant. Mechanically significant. Requires professional handling. | Journeyman |
| Intense | 8-10 | 3-4 | Powerful, potentially area-affecting. Dangerous without expert containment. | Master |

### The Bottlenecking Principle

In any combined state, the lowest-quality component limits the overall expression. A Formation Stage 8 mutation in a Formation Stage 4 material can only express at the substrate's capacity. The mutation's potential is 8; its actual output is capped at 4. The material is the bottleneck.

### Heritage States

Every material's planar expression exists in one of three heritage states. These do not modify target numbers — they determine which operations are applicable.

**Active:** The process is ongoing. The expression is still changing, still responsive to input. Lava is Active — it's still flowing, still cooling, still reacting with its environment. Active materials accept new influences more readily.

**Arrested:** The process was interrupted. The expression carries tension from the interruption — it was going somewhere and was stopped mid-journey. Tempered steel is Arrested — the quench froze the crystalline structure in a metastable state that carries the energy of the interrupted cooling process. Arrested materials carry properties that neither the starting nor the ending state would have.

**Settled:** The process reached completion. The Synod has claimed it. The expression is at its most stable final state and resists change.Ite is Settled — geological time has driven it to equilibrium. Settled materials are the most stable and the most resistant to modification.

---

## READING MATERIALS — THE DUAL PERIODIC TABLE

Every material in the system is read from two periodic tables simultaneously.

### The Real Periodic Table — Physical Properties

The material's physical characteristics, measured on 1-10 scales derived from real-world measurements:

| Property | Real Basis | Scale | Feeds Into |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hardness** | Mohs scale (direct 1-10) | Talc (1) to Diamond (10) | Protection Effect Rank, Duration contribution |
| **Tensile Strength** | Normalized MPa | Clay (1) to Ultra-high alloys (10) | Damage Effect Rank |
| **Density** | Normalized g/cm³ | Cork (1) to Osmium (10) | Damage bonus (mass), Range modifier (inverse — heavy = short range, light = long range) |
| **Conductivity** | Combined thermal/electrical | Dry wood (1) to Superconductor (10) | Chain tendency, energy throughput, Range through conductive paths |
| **Elasticity** | Inverted Young's modulus | Diamond (1) to Perfect elasticity (10) | Affliction (binding/grapple), Deflect effectiveness, Toggleable tendency |

**Material Flags** — binary properties that gate specific behaviors:

| Flag | Effect |
|---|---|
| Brittle | Favors Burst/Spread geometry. Natural Split tendency. Feedback risk. |
| Ductile | Favors Line/Cone geometry. Stretching expressions. |
| Porous | Favors Spread/Emanation geometry. Lingering Area tendency. |
| Conductive | Favors Chain targeting. Extends range through conductive paths. |
| Malleable | Favors Emanation/Wall geometry. Deforms to fill space. |
| Rigid | Favors Wall/Ray geometry. Directional, structured expressions. |

### The Alchemical Periodic Table — Planar Properties

The material's planar identity:

- **Plane of origin** — which planar engine the material's expression derives from
- **Isotope identity** — which specific isotope state the material embodies
- **Formation Stage** — how intensely the planar expression has crystallized (1-10)
- **Heritage State** — Active, Arrested, or Settled
- **Interaction Profile** — Static, Cycling, Projecting, or Absorbing

### Hazard Ratings — NFPA 704 Alignment

Every material carries three hazard ratings scaled 1-10 from real NFPA 704 data:

| Rating | NFPA Equivalent | What It Measures |
|---|---|---|
| **Health Hazard** | Blue quadrant | All biological harm — acute toxicity, corrosion, respiratory damage, chronic organ effects. Imposes penalty on crafting rolls unless mitigated by protection. |
| **Physical Hazard** | Red quadrant | Flammability, combustibility, thermal energy release potential. Imposes penalty unless mitigated by containment. |
| **Instability** | Yellow quadrant | Chemical reactivity, tendency toward violent decomposition, detonation potential. Imposes penalty unless mitigated by containment. |

### GHS Hazard Flags

In addition to the three ratings, materials carry specific hazard flags from the GHS classification system:

| Flag | What It Means |
|---|---|
| **Mutagenic** | Triggers the Planar Strain system on exposure. GHS Germ Cell Mutagenicity. |
| **Carcinogenic** | Triggers V-13 Cancer-equivalent condition track degradation on chronic exposure. |
| **Corrosive (Contact)** | Deals Physical condition track damage on skin contact. |
| **Corrosive (Metallic)** | Deals Physical condition track damage to metal equipment. |
| **Corrosive (Ocular)** | Deals Physical condition track damage to vision specifically. |
| **Respiratory Hazard** | Deals Physical condition track damage through inhalation. |
| **Water-Reactive** | Cooperative combination toward combustion/detonation on water contact. |
| **Oxidizer** | Increases effective Physical Hazard rating of adjacent flammable materials. |
| **Pyrophoric** | Spontaneous ignition on air contact. No ignition source needed. |
| **Pressurized** | Rupture hazard. Mechanical damage on containment failure. |
| **Bioaccumulative** | Substance accumulates in biological tissue. Chronic exposure damage cannot be healed by rest — tracked through the Strain system. |
| **Aspiration Hazard** | Liquid entering lungs through ingestion or vomiting. |

### Reading a Material — Example

**Steel Longsword (Formation Stage 5, Arrested Heritage)**

| Property | Rating |
|---|---|
| Hardness | 6 |
| Tensile Strength | 6 |
| Density | 6 |
| Conductivity | 5 |
| Elasticity | 2 |

Flags: Conductive, Ductile
Heritage: Arrested (tempered — the quench froze the crystalline structure)
Plane: Borderlands
Isotope: Combined state (iron + carbon synthesis — primarily B-09 Bedrock character with B-10 Magma processing heritage)
NFPA: Health 0 / Physical 1 / Instability 0
GHS Flags: None

This stat block tells you everything about how the sword interacts with the spell sentence system. Its Tensile Strength 6 feeds Damage Effect Rank. Its Hardness 6 feeds Protection and Duration. Its Ductile flag favors Line/Cone geometry for channeled expressions. Its Conductivity 5 enables Chain targeting for energy expressions routed through it.

---

## THE FOUR-LAYER ITEM MODEL

Every item has up to four distinct layers. These are separate in origin, function, and mechanical behavior.

### Layer 1: Base Material

The physical substance. Its properties derive from the real periodic table — physical stats on 1-10 scales, material flags, hazard ratings. The base material also carries an alchemical passport identifying its planar heritage and compatibility. The base material's Tier (derived from its physical properties as potentially modified by mutations) determines the item's architectural capacity — how many slots it has and what EML ceiling it can support.

### Layer 2: Expressions (Mutations and Enchantments)

Planar influences combined into or imposed upon the substance. Mutations occur through natural exposure or environmental process. Enchantments are deliberately imposed by a practitioner. Both are mechanically identical — the distinction is origin, not mechanism. Expressions generate their own charges through the drip recharge system and count as Active Expressions toward the item's EML.

### Layer 3: Attachments

Sub-items carrying rotes, installed in the parent item's slots. Each attachment is a complete item with its own material reading, its own charge pool, and its own condition tracks. Attachments are portable between compatible housings. They contribute their material properties to any spell routed through them.

### Layer 4: Insets

Spell sentence modifiers installed in slots. Each inset corresponds to an Additive or Subtractive from the spell sentence system, made into a physical component. Insets modify rotes or channeled spells that pass through their position in the routing architecture. They generate charges as a secondary function of their material.

The **battery** is not a separate layer — the base material generates its own charges naturally. A dedicated battery component can be installed in a regular slot as a trade-off (more power budget, one fewer capability slot).

---

## MASTERY AND SCALE

### The 1-10 Mortal Scale

Mastery is rated 1-10 for mortal beings and items. Bands align with proficiency thresholds:

| Band | Range | Character | Crafting Access |
|---|---|---|---|
| Novice | 1-4 | Learning, developing | Formation Stage 1-4 materials |
| Journeyman | 5-7 | Professional, trained | Formation Stage 5-7 materials |
| Master | 8-10 | Expert, peak mortal capability | Formation Stage 8-10 materials |

### Item EML

An item's Effective Mastery Level reflects its current magical expression:

**EML = Highest Active Expression + floor((Total Active Expressions − 1) ÷ 4)**

Active Expressions include the item's own mutations/enchantments AND each attachment's rote. Insets, batteries, and Forge Links are not Active Expressions.

Material Tier caps EML — a Tier 6 material cannot support EML above 6 regardless of installed expressions.

### Material Properties and the Spell Sentence

An item's physical properties feed directly into the spell sentence parameters for any expression it carries or any spell channeled through it. The material is not neutral housing — it is an active participant in every magical expression it supports.

| Property | Spell Sentence Parameter |
|---|---|
| Hardness | Protection Effect Rank, Duration |
| Tensile Strength | Damage Effect Rank |
| Density | Damage bonus + Range (inverse) |
| Conductivity | Chain, energy throughput, Range through conductors |
| Elasticity | Affliction (binding), Deflect, Toggleable |
| Material Flags | Gate specific geometry options and natural Additive/Subtractive tendencies |

---

## KEY VOCABULARY

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Prima Materia** | The framework governing the five planar engines and their interactions with matter, biology, technology, narrative, and entropy |
| **Isotope** | A specific planar expression defined by plane, phase composition, and interaction profile. Learned as individual talents ranked 1-10 |
| **Formation Stage** | 1-10 rating of the intensity at which a material's planar expression crystallized. Aligned to NFPA/GHS hazard bands |
| **Heritage State** | Active (ongoing process), Arrested (interrupted, carries tension), or Settled (completed, Synod-claimed, resists change) |
| **Interaction Profile** | How a material's phase state behaves: Static (Solid), Cycling (Liquid), Projecting (Gas), Absorbing (Plasma) |
| **Alchemical Passport** | The complete reading of a material's planar identity — heritage, compatibility, Formation Stage, and planar participation |
| **Expression** | A planar influence in a material — encompassing both mutations (natural) and enchantments (deliberate). Same mechanism, different origin |
| **Rote** | A complete spell sentence in a specific fixed configuration. Produces the same expression every time |
| **Attachment** | A portable sub-item carrying one rote, installed in a parent item's slot. Has its own material properties and charge pool |
| **Inset** | A spell sentence modifier installed in a slot. Corresponds to a specific Additive or Subtractive |
| **CML** | Component Mastery Level — the power rating of a single expression or installed rote |
| **EML** | Effective Mastery Level — the overall power rating of an item, derived from its active expressions |
| **Catalyzed Diagonal** | A combination of competing phase states within the same plane, requiring sustained Forge mediation |
| **Emulsion** | A combination of expressions from antithetical planes, requiring Forge catalyst |
| **Drip Recharge** | The system by which charges recover over time, anchored to the 10-minute base interval |
| **Mastery** | 1-10 rating of capability; 10 is peak mortal. The governing scale for both characters and items |
| **Wraith (S-09)** | Synod Spectral Pure isotope — spectral persistence of identity beyond physical function |
| **Pneuma** | The Hidden Forge Gas phase — directed pressure infrastructure |
| **Cooperative / Competing / Cancellation / Emergent** | The four behavioral outcomes when planar expressions interact |
| **Synthesis / Decomposition / Displacement** | The three fundamental alchemical operations |
| **Health Hazard / Physical Hazard / Instability** | The three NFPA-aligned hazard ratings, each scaled 1-10 |
| **Planar Strain** | Adaptation pressure from planar exposure, tracked per isotope. Drives the mutation system |
| **Calcification** | A mutation whose XP debt is fully cleared. Permanent baseline. Settled Heritage State |

# THE ÆTHERIUM CODEX — System Reference Document
## Section II: The Dual Periodic Table — Reading Materials

*Alpha SRD Draft — Working Document*

---

### PREAMBLE: THE SHARED LANGUAGE

The real periodic table and the alchemical periodic table use the same descriptive language. This is not a coincidence — the alchemical system was derived from observing real materials and processes. When the B-10 Magma isotope profile describes "adhesive thermal contact, self-sealing, persistent heat, mass behind the burn," it is describing what real magma actually does. When that same isotope expresses through a different medium — bone, wood, paper, silk — the carrier material exhibits those same behaviors through its own substance.

An alchemist looking at flowing lava and an alchemist looking at Magma-mutated bone are reading the same behavioral signatures expressed through different substrates. The isotope profile is the shared vocabulary. The base material is the medium. The mutation makes the medium behave like the profile describes, without the medium ceasing to be what it is.

This shared language means that a practitioner trained in real chemistry can read alchemical profiles and immediately understand what they're looking at, because the descriptions map to observable physical phenomena. A practitioner trained in alchemy can predict how a real material will behave, because the same vocabulary applies. The two readings are different — one measures what the material IS physically, the other measures what planar expression it carries — but they speak the same language.

---

### PART I: READING ONE — THE REAL PERIODIC TABLE

The first reading determines the material's physical identity. This is non-magical. It is what the substance is, measured by the same properties a real-world chemist or materials scientist would assess.

**Physical Properties → System Statistics**

Every base material translates to system statistics through direct measurement of its real-world properties:

| Real Property | Measurement | System Statistic | Scale |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hardness | Mohs scale (1-10) | Soak contribution | Direct — Mohs maps to Soak |
| Tensile strength | Megapascals (MPa) | Damage tier | Tiered — MPa ranges map to damage dice |
| Density | g/cm³ | Weight class | Light / Standard / Heavy |
| Melting point | °C/°F | Heat tolerance | Determines crafting thresholds |
| Conductivity | Electrical and thermal | Behavioral tags | Conductive / Insulative |
| Elasticity | Young's modulus | Flexibility tag | Rigid / Flexible / Elastic |

These are not abstractions. Steel has a tensile strength of approximately 400-550 MPa, which places it in the 1d8 damage tier. Obsidian has a Mohs hardness of 5-5.5, which gives it a moderate Soak contribution. Bone has a density of approximately 1.9 g/cm³, making it Light weight class. The real numbers produce the system numbers.

**Material-Specific Flags**

Some materials carry mechanical flags derived from specific physical characteristics that don't fit the standard stat categories:

- **Brittle:** The material fractures rather than deforming under stress. High edge sharpness but vulnerable to impact. Obsidian, glass, cast iron, ceramic.
- **Malleable:** The material deforms under compression without fracturing. Can be reshaped through processing. Gold, copper, lead, clay.
- **Ductile:** The material can be drawn into wire or stretched without breaking. Applicable to tensile applications. Steel, copper, silver.
- **Porous:** The material has internal structure with voids. Can absorb or be permeated by liquids and compounds. Bone, wood, unglazed ceramic, pumice.
- **Conductive:** The material transfers thermal or electrical energy efficiently. Copper, silver, iron, water (with dissolved salts).
- **Insulative:** The material resists energy transfer. Glass, ceramic, dry wood, silk.

A material may carry multiple flags. Steel is both Ductile and Conductive. Bone is both Porous and Brittle at thin cross-sections.

**Chemical Behavior Tags**

Every material carries one or more tags describing its chemical behavior — how it interacts with other substances during processing, use, and environmental exposure:

- **Corrosive:** Dissolves or degrades other materials on contact through chemical reaction. Acids, concentrated bases, reactive salts. Relevant to processing operations involving dissolution and to item interactions where contacted equipment is at risk.
- **Caustic:** Damages biological tissue specifically. May or may not be corrosive to non-biological materials. Lye, strong acids, certain plant toxins. Relevant to handling hazards and to weapon/affliction applications.
- **Volatile:** Unstable under energy input. Prone to phase change, rapid decomposition, or energy release when heated, shocked, or compressed. Nitroglycerin, phosphorus, alkali metals. Relevant to crafting safety and storage requirements.
- **Inert:** Minimal chemical reactivity. Resists corrosion, oxidation, and combination. Gold, glass, platinum, noble gases. Relevant to stability — inert materials persist without degradation but are also resistant to being chemically modified.
- **Reactive:** Readily combines with other substances under appropriate conditions. Iron (oxidizes), sodium (combusts in water), carbon (forms compounds with nearly everything). Not inherently dangerous but indicates the material will participate actively in chemical operations.
- **Toxic:** Causes systemic damage to biological organisms through exposure over time, distinct from Caustic contact damage. Mercury, arsenic, lead, certain fungal compounds. Not neutralized by crafting skill — a master alchemist working with mercury still risks mercury poisoning. Magic can mitigate, treat, or circumvent toxicity, but the baseline danger is a property of the material.

A material may carry multiple chemical tags. Hydrochloric acid is Corrosive, Caustic, and Volatile. Mercury is Volatile and Toxic. Gold is Inert. Iron is Reactive (oxidizes readily). These tags feed directly into crafting requirements — Volatile materials require Forge Channeled infrastructure, Toxic materials require protective measures, Inert materials resist chemical processing operations.

---

### PART II: READING TWO — THE ALCHEMICAL PERIODIC TABLE

The second reading determines the material's planar identity. This is the alchemical passport — the record of what planar expressions the material carries and how those expressions behave.

**Planar Heritage**

Every material that has been exposed to planar influence carries a heritage — the record of which plane and which isotope expression has combined into its substance. Obsidian carries B-10 Magma heritage because it formed from a volcanic process that corresponds to the Borderlands Fire+Earth expression. Bone carries S-01 Grave heritage because it is the mineralized endpoint of a biological structure — the Synod's claim on organic matter made permanent.

Heritage is information. It tells the alchemist what went into the material and what it is compatible with. It does not by itself produce mechanical effects — that requires an active mutation.

**Heritage States**

Every material's heritage exists in one of three states, describing where the material sits in the process of reaching its final stable form:

**Active** — The originating process is still ongoing. The material is in the middle of its formation event. Molten lava, living wood, fresh blood, actively growing crystal. Active materials are more volatile but also more responsive to Forge processing — the practitioner is working with a process already in motion rather than trying to restart a completed one. Active heritage interacts readily with synthesis operations and is the easiest state to direct toward a specific outcome.

**Arrested** — The process was interrupted before reaching its final state. The material stopped in transit. Quenched steel (cooled rapidly before the crystal structure could fully settle), flash-frozen tissue (biological process halted mid-function), partially decayed remains (the Synod's claim begun but not completed). Arrested materials carry the tension of their interrupted process — they exist in a state the material did not choose and may cooperate or resist unpredictably during processing. This tension can be an asset (tempered steel is stronger than fully settled iron precisely because the arrest created a useful internal stress) or a liability (the material may try to resume its interrupted process at inopportune moments).

**Settled** — The process completed naturally and the material has reached its most stable state. Obsidian (magma cooled to glass), fossil bone (organic structure fully mineralized), fully oxidized iron (rust — the Synod's claim on iron completed). Settled materials are stable, predictable, and resistant to change. The Synod has already claimed them. Working a Settled material into something new means fighting that claim — applying enough Forge processing to overcome the stability the material has achieved. Settled materials have the highest crafting resistance but the most predictable behavior.

Heritage state affects crafting difficulty, processing options, and material behavior during the Artifice Engine phase. An Active Formation Stage 6 material presents a completely different crafting challenge than a Settled Formation Stage 6 material — same intensity of planar expression, entirely different working conditions.

**Formation Stage (1-10)**

Formation Stage measures the intensity at which the material's planar expression crystallized — how strongly the planar influence is embedded in the substance. The scale is 1 to 10, aligned with the system's standard 10-point scale.

| Formation Stage | Band | Description | Crafting Access |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1-2 | Low | Trace planar influence. Clean, stable samples. Common materials with ambient exposure. Effects are cosmetic or functionally minor. | Novice — any trained practitioner |
| 3-4 | Moderate | Clear planar expression. Distinct alchemical signature. Reliably produces measurable effects. The standard working range for most crafted items. | Novice-accessible with care |
| 5-6 | Strong | Pronounced planar expression. The material's behavior is noticeably shaped by its isotope. Effects are combat-relevant and functionally significant. Handling requires expertise. | Journeyman — requires trained skill |
| 7 | Intense | Strong enough to alter the material's physical properties beyond baseline. The planar expression is actively competing with or overriding the base material's natural behavior. | Journeyman (advanced) |
| 8-9 | Extreme | Dangerously intense planar expression. The material may be hazardous to handle without protection. The planar influence dominates the physical substrate. Effects are powerful and potentially difficult to control. | Master — requires mastery-level skill |
| 10 | Absolute | Peak planar crystallization. The material is as strongly influenced as a substance can be while remaining a physical material rather than becoming a pure planar manifestation. Theoretical ceiling. | Master (peak) — the limit of mortal crafting |

Formation Stage is independent of Heritage State. A Formation Stage 8 Active material (an extremely intense ongoing process) and a Formation Stage 8 Settled material (an extremely intense completed process) both have the same intensity of planar expression but present completely different crafting situations.

Formation Stage is also independent of Material Tier. Tier measures physical properties. Formation Stage measures planar intensity. A Formation Stage 8 piece of paper is still physically paper — unless its mutations have altered its physical properties, in which case the effective Tier reflects the combined state, not the Formation Stage alone.

**Interaction Profile**

Every mutation's behavior is characterized by its phase-derived interaction profile:

- **Static (Solid-derived):** The expression persists without change. It holds position, maintains state, resists modification. Bedrock, Osseous, Crystal, Echo.
- **Cycling (Liquid-derived):** The expression transforms what passes through it. It converts, processes, redistributes, dissolves. Water, Archival, Chem, Veil.
- **Projecting (Gas-derived):** The expression pushes outward. It broadcasts, disperses, radiates, expands. Air, Spectral, Pneuma, Probability.
- **Absorbing (Plasma-derived):** The expression draws inward. It consumes, converts fuel, self-sustains through intake. Fire, Void, Volt, Glitch.

In compound isotopes, the dominant phase sets the primary profile and the recessive phase adds behavioral character. B-10 Magma (Solid dominant, Plasma recessive) is primarily Static with Absorbing character — it persists (stone) while consuming (fire). This profile determines how the mutation behaves when expressed through any base material.

---

### PART III: CHEMICAL INTERACTIONS — THE FOUR OUTCOMES

When planar expressions interact — whether through natural exposure, deliberate alchemical processing, or environmental conditions — the interaction produces one of four outcomes. None of these outcomes are inherently good or bad. They are tools. The alchemist's craft is knowing which outcome to target and how to achieve it.

**Cooperative Combination**

The two expressions reinforce or complement each other, producing a combined state with properties neither had alone — analogous to alloying. Iron plus carbon produces steel: harder and more flexible than either constituent. A base material with compatible mutations produces an emergent combined profile read as a single substance with enhanced or hybrid properties.

Cooperative combinations occur naturally between adjacent expressions on the phase wheel and between compatible planar expressions. They are stable without requiring sustained input.

**Competing Combination**

The two expressions actively resist each other, each trying to resolve the combined state toward its own dominance — analogous to an emulsion under agitation. Fire and water on the same material: each is trying to annihilate the other. The result is either dominance (one expression wins and suppresses the other), contested equilibrium (both persist in unstable tension requiring sustained energy to maintain), or oscillation (the material cycles between expressing one and then the other).

Competing combinations correspond to the catalyzed diagonals within a single plane. They can exist in nature wherever the environment provides the sustained input — but they are not self-sustaining without that input.

**Cancellation**

The two expressions destroy each other's contributions, producing a net reduction — analogous to neutralization in acid-base chemistry. The acid and base don't combine into something stronger; they produce a salt and water, both less reactive than either input. Two antithetical planar expressions meeting without Forge mediation on the same material can strip both expressions, leaving the base material with reduced or eliminated planar identity.

Cancellation is a deliberate tool. An alchemist who needs to remove an unwanted mutation can introduce a cancelling expression to neutralize it. The result is a material with less planar influence, which may be exactly what the situation requires.

**Emergent Properties**

The combination produces effects that exist on neither constituent's isotope profile — analogous to how water has properties (liquid at room temperature, surface tension, universal solvent behavior) that neither hydrogen nor oxygen possess. This is the generative outcome: the interaction created something new that could not have been predicted from the individual profiles alone.

Emergent properties are the most valuable and least predictable outcome. They are why master alchemists experiment with combinations that don't have established results — the potential for discovery of genuinely new effects.

---

### PART IV: THE THREE OPERATIONS

Crafters apply three fundamental operations to materials to produce the four interaction outcomes. These are the active verbs of the alchemy system — the things a practitioner actually does.

**Synthesis**

Combining two or more substances or expressions to produce a new combined state. Adding a reagent carrying a specific isotope expression to a base material. Introducing a catalyst to force two competing expressions into coexistence. Alloying metals. Brewing compound potions from multiple reagents.

Synthesis is additive. Something goes in. The result is a combined state with emergent, cooperative, or competing properties depending on the compatibility of the inputs.

**Decomposition**

Breaking a combined state apart into simpler constituents or products. Stripping a mutation from a material. Separating an alloy back into component metals. Distilling a compound into fractions. Using cancellation deliberately to neutralize a specific expression.

Decomposition is subtractive. Something is taken apart. The result is simpler components that may each be useful independently or as inputs for subsequent synthesis operations.

**Displacement**

Replacing one constituent with another without fully decomposing the whole. Introducing a stronger planar expression that ejects a weaker one from the compound. Swapping one mutation for another by introducing a reagent that outcompetes the existing expression for the material's "chemical space."

Displacement is substitutive. Something is swapped. The original constituent is ejected (potentially recoverable as a precipitate) and the new constituent takes its place in the compound. The overall structure is preserved but its character changes.

---

### PART V: THE COMBINED STATE — READING THE RESULT

When a material has undergone chemical interaction — whether through natural processes, environmental exposure, or deliberate crafting — the alchemist reads the RESULT, not the individual layers. Water is measured as water, not as "hydrogen with an oxygen mutation." Steel is measured as steel, not as "iron with a carbon additive." The combination has produced something with its own properties.

This means the material tables describe combined states as an alchemist encounters them. The base elements table tells you what went into common materials. The combined materials table tells you what those materials actually ARE — their effective properties as a single substance after all interactions have resolved.

An Analyzer examining a finished item reads the current combined state and can reverse-engineer the recipe — identifying the base material, the mutations that have been synthesized into it, the attachments applied at its surfaces, the enchantments imposed on its properties, and the insets socketed into its architecture. This is the full five-layer reading:

1. **Base compounds:** The chemical composition of the substance (H₂O, NaCl, iron-carbon alloy)
2. **Mutations:** Planar expressions chemically combined into the substance
3. **Attachments:** Alchemical treatments at contact boundaries
4. **Enchantments:** Field effects amplifying or modifying existing properties
5. **Insets:** Modular magical devices socketed into the item's architecture

The first three are chemistry. The fourth is field imposition (analogous to electromagnetism). The fifth is mechanical interface. Each layer is distinct in mechanism, permanence, and power source.

---

### PART VI: MATERIAL TIER — A DERIVED STATISTIC

Material Tier is the item's effective physical capability — hardness, structural integrity, density, and the physical limits of what the item can support. Tier determines the number of inset slots available and the maximum EML the item's architecture can sustain.

Tier is DERIVED, not fixed. It starts from the base material's physical properties but is modified by any mutations that alter those physical properties. Paper has a low base Tier. Paper with a B-09 Bedrock mutation expressing at Formation Stage 5 has an effective Tier reflecting the hardness and structural integrity the Bedrock expression has imparted. The paper hasn't ceased to be paper — but its physical properties have been altered by the chemical combination with the Bedrock expression.

Mutations can stack. A material can carry multiple mutations, each contributing to the combined state's effective physical properties. The Tier reflects the sum result — the material as it IS now, after all chemical interactions have resolved.

Tier can decrease as well as increase. A mutation that makes a material more brittle, lighter, or less structurally coherent lowers the effective Tier. Cancellation of a beneficial mutation reverts the Tier toward the base material's floor.

| Tier | Slots | Physical Character | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 0 | Mundane — no magical architecture supported | Unmodified iron, common wood, basic leather |
| 2 | 2 | Minor structural enhancement; supports simple magical components | Mithril, heartwood, spirit-touched materials |
| 4 | 4 | Standard adventuring grade; reliable under combat stress | Dragon bone, planar crystal, force-metal |
| 6 | 6 | Superior — notable physical properties beyond mundane limits | Primordial iron, god-touched steel |
| 8 | 8 | Exceptional — regionally famous relics | Stellar metal, demigod-crafted materials |
| 10 | 10 | Peak mortal — the limit of what mortal craftsmanship can produce | The finest materials at the highest Formation Stages |

---

### PART VII: VOLATILITY AND REACTIVITY IN CRAFTING

**Volatility** measures how difficult the material is to work safely — its tendency to change state, release energy, or behave unpredictably under crafting conditions. Volatility adds directly to the Crafting Target Number. High-volatility materials require Forge Channeled infrastructure (containment, pressure management, temperature control) to process safely.

**Reactivity** measures how readily the material participates in chemical operations — its tendency to combine, corrode, oxidize, or react with other substances. High reactivity means more processing pathways are available but also more potential failure modes. Low reactivity (Inert materials) means fewer available operations but more predictable behavior.

These are separate axes. A material can be:

- **High Volatility, High Reactivity:** Dangerous and active. Many processing options but serious safety requirements. Alkali metals, concentrated magical reagents.
- **High Volatility, Low Reactivity:** Dangerous but chemically stubborn. Hard to work safely AND hard to modify. Nitroglycerin (unstable but doesn't corrode tools).
- **Low Volatility, High Reactivity:** Safe to handle but chemically aggressive. Easy working conditions with strong processing potential. Concentrated acids in appropriate containers.
- **Low Volatility, Low Reactivity:** Safe and stable. Limited processing options. Gold, glass, platinum.

**Toxicity** is tracked separately from both Volatility and Reactivity. A Toxic material imposes biological hazard on the practitioner through exposure — inhalation, skin absorption, or ingestion — regardless of crafting skill level. Toxicity is not mitigated by expertise. A master alchemist working with mercury still risks mercury poisoning. Protective measures (Forge Channeled containment, alchemical treatments, magical shielding) can reduce exposure, and healing magic can treat the effects, but the baseline danger is a property of the material.

---

### PART VIII: COMMON BASE MATERIALS TABLE

The following table covers materials that compose approximately 90% of crafted items. Each entry reflects the material's real-world physical properties translated to system statistics. Planar heritage and mutations are listed where common environmental exposure typically produces them, but any material may carry any mutation depending on its specific history.

| Material | Hardness | Damage Tier | Weight | Heritage State | Typical Formation Stage | Chemical Tags | Flags | Common Planar Heritage |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Iron | Moderate (4) | 1d6 | Standard | Settled (ore→smelted→cooled) | 1-3 | Reactive (oxidizes) | Conductive, Malleable | B-09 Bedrock / B-10 Magma |
| Steel | High (6-7) | 1d8 | Standard | Arrested (tempered/quenched) | 2-4 | Low Reactive | Conductive, Ductile | B-09/Forge (CP-10 alloy process) |
| Copper | Moderate (3) | 1d4 | Standard | Settled or Arrested | 1-3 | Reactive (oxidizes, verdigris) | Conductive, Malleable, Ductile | B-14 Current affinity |
| Bronze | Moderate-High (5) | 1d6 | Standard | Arrested (alloy) | 2-4 | Low Reactive | Malleable | Emergent alloy properties |
| Tin | Low (2) | 1d4 | Light | Settled | 1-2 | Low Reactive | Malleable | — |
| Silver | Moderate (3) | 1d4 | Standard | Settled or Arrested | 1-3 | Low Reactive | Conductive, Malleable, Ductile | Synod affinity (traditional) |
| Gold | Low (2.5) | 1d4 | Heavy | Settled | 1-4 | Inert | Conductive, Malleable, Ductile | Varies — Inert resists planar fixing |
| Obsidian | Moderate (5) | 1d6 (edge quality) | Light | Settled (magma→glass) | 1-3 | Inert | Brittle, Insulative | B-10 Magma heritage |

| Lead | Low (1.5) | 1d4 | Very Heavy | Settled | 1-2 | Low Reactive | Malleable, Toxic | — |
